Multi-factor authentication and key agreement scheme based on PUF and Chebyshev chaotic map for wireless sensor networks
Authors: Linjie Wang, Chunxia Han
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
Wireless sensor networks (WSNs) communication systems represent a flexible, reliable, and efficient paradigm that enables bidirectional real-time data exchange, as well as mutual identity authentication between the User and the Sensor Node. However, due to the open nature of these networks, communications among entities may be intercepted or manipulated by attackers through public channels at any time. As WSNs often lack fundamental security mechanisms, they are vulnerable to various potential threats. In response to these challenges, we propose a three-party authentication and key agreement scheme for WSNs based on physical unclonable functions (PUFs) and the lightweight Chebyshev chaotic map cryptographic operation. This approach ensures the preservation of privacy and resilience against machine learning-based modeling attacks, as well as other existing security risks. Furthermore, by incorporating hash functions and XOR operations into the framework design, the pseudonyms of Users and the parameters used for identity authentication are dynamically generated and updated, achieving one-time usage. The correctness and security of the proposed scheme are validated through both formal and informal analysis methods. Additionally, a simulation of practical deployment scenarios is conducted to evaluate its computational and communication overhead in comparison with competing schemes. The detailed comparative results demonstrate that the proposed scheme outperforms existing approaches in terms of efficiency and security.

Introduction

The rapid development of artificial intelligence (AI) fundamentally depends on the availability of large-scale data resources. In this context, sensor nodes, as essential components for data acquisition, play an increasingly critical role. These sensor-equipped devices are inherently heterogeneous in terms of their architectural design, interconnection methods, and operational environments1. Consequently, wireless sensor networks (WSNs), which facilitate the connectivity of such nodes, have been widely deployed across various domains. Given that substantial volumes of sensitive information are transmitted over public communication channels, WSNs are regarded as a core element of the Internet of Things (IoT). As a groundbreaking technological advancement in this highly interconnected era, IoT enables seamless communication with ubiquitous sensor-integrated devices2, thereby making the security of such communications a focal point for researchers. However, due to the nature of wireless transmission, WSNs operating within IoT environments are particularly vulnerable to multiple types of security threats, including passive eavesdropping, active intrusion, and message replay attacks3. Therefore, addressing the security vulnerabilities inherent in IoT-based wireless sensor networks has become an urgent research imperative4.

Wireless sensor nodes transmit the collected and monitored data to the base station for storage and processing, thereby enabling real-time data services for a wide range of applications. Despite their extensive deployment, WSNs face several limitations, including constrained resources of sensor nodes, insecure communication channels, and susceptibility to unauthorized access5. These constraints increase the vulnerability of WSNs to targeted attacks, posing significant security risks to network participants. To prevent information leakage and ensure the authenticity and integrity of data exchanged in open network environments, robust identity authentication mechanisms are essential. However, as authentication procedures typically occur over public channels, they remain exposed to threats such as eavesdropping and replay attacks6,7. Therefore, an “Authentication and Key Agreement” (AKA) scheme not only facilitates secure mutual authentication but also serves as a critical safeguard for data privacy8. Considering the resource-constrained nature of sensor nodes, authentication protocols must also be computationally efficient. By leveraging the semigroup properties of Chebyshev polynomials, a symmetric key system can be designed to achieve high computational efficiency in encryption and decryption processes. Consequently, authentication protocols incorporating Chebyshev polynomials offer enhanced security with minimal computational overhead, making them particularly well-suited for deployment in wireless sensor networks9.

Related works

In recent years, significant advancements have been made in sensor design; however, security remains a critical concern. Numerous security solutions have been proposed to address this issue and enhance the security of WSNs10. Wong et al.11 developed an authentication scheme that evenly distributes the computational load across all nodes in WSNs by utilizing dynamic passwords. Subsequently, Das et al.12 proposed an enhanced authentication scheme based on smart cards and passwords to resist impersonation attacks, stolen-verifier attacks, and replay attacks. Building upon Das et al.’s scheme, several research studies have been conducted13–15. While these approaches address certain limitations of Das et al.’s scheme, they fail to provide resistance against offline password guessing attacks, thereby introducing new vulnerabilities. To address these shortcomings, researchers16–18 have proposed authentication protocols based on biometric features, leading to the development of a novel security-enhanced scheme for WSNs within the IoT context.

Lightweight authentication protocols are widely adopted in environments characterized by limited hardware resources and computational capabilities. Turkanović et al.19 proposed an authentication protocol that employs only hash functions and XOR operations, enabling remote users to securely negotiate session keys with sensor nodes at low computational cost. Pommbage et al.20 introduced a protocol designed to maintain symmetric links among sensor nodes under conditions of limited energy and computational capacity, thereby facilitating lightweight key exchange. To better suit industrial IoT environments, Esfahani et al.21 developed a machine-to-machine communication authentication protocol based on hash and XOR operations, which achieves relatively low computational and communication overhead. Fatima et al.22 proposed a lightweight three-factor authentication protocol with adaptive privacy protection by integrating lightweight encryption primitives with the Chinese Remainder Theorem. Aiming to improve both security and efficiency, Thakur et al.23 proposed a novel three-factor authentication scheme that fully leverages the advantages of fuzzy extractors and cryptographic one-way hash functions.

In 2024, Huang24 combined biometrics, smart cards, and password authentication technology to propose a new three-factor authentication and key negotiation solution for wireless sensor networks based on elliptic curve cryptography (ECC). However, the scheme was too complex and inefficient. To address the shortcomings of previous solutions, Gao et al.25 proposed a highly available authentication and key negotiation protocol that conceals the true identities of IoT devices. This protocol reduces the computational and communication costs, and also takes into account the dynamic updates of the devices; however, this scheme cannot deal with ephemeral key leakage attacks. To counter physical threats, Zhou et al.26 utilized physical unclonable functions (PUFs) and proposed an improved authentication key exchange protocol in combination with biometric technology, fuzzy extractors, and secret salt values. The proposed protocol increases the difficulty of password guessing attacks and employs a formal and heuristic security analysis method to verify its robustness. However, a critical analysis indicates that although the protocol improves security and sustains operational efficiency, it may encounter challenges in large-scale deployment scenarios and in defending against sophisticated physical attacks27.

Motivation, contributions and outline

Motivation

This paper primarily seeks to significantly enhance data privacy during interactions between users and sensor nodes while leveraging state-of-the-art privacy protection technologies such as encryption, anonymization, and access control mechanisms. ensuring the robustness of data transmission security within WSNs, thus achieving strong authentication and key agreement. While current security solutions adeptly mitigate common threats such as man-in-the-middle attacks, eavesdropping, and replay attacks, they frequently encounter difficulties in defending against advanced threats, including those that leverage machine learning (ML) for modeling attacks. We leverage biometrics, passwords, and PUFs, utilizing lightweight Chebyshev chaotic maps to achieve secure mutual authentication and key agreement, as demonstrated in recent research on authentication protocols in wireless sensor networks. Given the critical vulnerability of static secret values, which are frequently exposed during authentication, we propose a novel scheme that facilitates dynamic mutual authentication between Users and Sensors orchestrated by the GWN, thereby substantially bolstering resilience against potential attacks.

Our contributions

A sophisticated physical security architecture is designed based on PUF by integrating lightweight Chebyshev chaotic mapping techniques, which enables the generation of one-time pseudorandom identifiers. By embedding the distinct properties of PUF into both Users and Sensors, the architecture achieves efficient, lightweight mapping, accelerated computational performance, and improved effectiveness in converting a given challenge value into a unique corresponding response.The proposed architecture incorporates a three-factor authentication mechanism that utilizes one-time pseudo identities to enable dynamic identity verification. Specifically, during the authentication process, access is granted exclusively to entities that possess both the correct pseudo identity and its corresponding credentials.Concerning session key security, the scheme undergoes rigorous security proofs and formal scheme analysis based on the Real-Or-Random (ROR) model and Mao-Boyd logic. Furthermore, extensive performance evaluations are conducted in terms of computational overhead, communication costs, and security capabilities, and these are compared against existing Authenticated Key Agreement (AKA) schemes.

A sophisticated physical security architecture is designed based on PUF by integrating lightweight Chebyshev chaotic mapping techniques, which enables the generation of one-time pseudorandom identifiers. By embedding the distinct properties of PUF into both Users and Sensors, the architecture achieves efficient, lightweight mapping, accelerated computational performance, and improved effectiveness in converting a given challenge value into a unique corresponding response.

A sophisticated physical security architecture is designed based on PUF by integrating lightweight Chebyshev chaotic mapping techniques, which enables the generation of one-time pseudorandom identifiers. By embedding the distinct properties of PUF into both Users and Sensors, the architecture achieves efficient, lightweight mapping, accelerated computational performance, and improved effectiveness in converting a given challenge value into a unique corresponding response.

The proposed architecture incorporates a three-factor authentication mechanism that utilizes one-time pseudo identities to enable dynamic identity verification. Specifically, during the authentication process, access is granted exclusively to entities that possess both the correct pseudo identity and its corresponding credentials.

The proposed architecture incorporates a three-factor authentication mechanism that utilizes one-time pseudo identities to enable dynamic identity verification. Specifically, during the authentication process, access is granted exclusively to entities that possess both the correct pseudo identity and its corresponding credentials.

Concerning session key security, the scheme undergoes rigorous security proofs and formal scheme analysis based on the Real-Or-Random (ROR) model and Mao-Boyd logic. Furthermore, extensive performance evaluations are conducted in terms of computational overhead, communication costs, and security capabilities, and these are compared against existing Authenticated Key Agreement (AKA) schemes.

Concerning session key security, the scheme undergoes rigorous security proofs and formal scheme analysis based on the Real-Or-Random (ROR) model and Mao-Boyd logic. Furthermore, extensive performance evaluations are conducted in terms of computational overhead, communication costs, and security capabilities, and these are compared against existing Authenticated Key Agreement (AKA) schemes.

Outline of the paper

The rest of this article is structured as follows: In Section “Preliminaries”, the basic concepts and mathematical preliminaries for WSNs are introduced. In Section “System model and adversary model”, the adversary model and design goals are introduced. In Section “Proposed scheme”, the detailed proposed framework of the authentication process is presented. Sections “Security proof and analysis” and “Performance analysis” present the theoretical and formal security analysis as well as performance analysis, respectively. Finally, the last Section “Conclusions” draws the conclusion and future work.

Preliminaries

Definition and characters of Chebyshev chaotic map

Chebyshev chaotic map is lightweight and highly efficient in parallel computing, making it ideal for building authentication schemes in IoT application scenarios where device resources are limited28. Below, we briefly present the definition and properties of the Chebyshev chaotic map.

Definition 1

(Chebyshev polynomial): Given n a positive integer, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in \left[ { - 1,1} \right]$$\end{document}, and the n-degree x polynomial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\Gamma _n}\left( x \right) :\left[ { - 1,1} \right] \rightarrow \left[ { - 1,1} \right]$$\end{document} is defined as Chebyshev polynomial which can be expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\Gamma _n}\left( x \right) = \cos \left( {n\;\arccos \left( x \right) } \right)$$\end{document}, and it satisfies the following properties:

Physical unclonable function (PUF)

A PUF is fundamentally a one-way function that is often regarded as the digital fingerprint of a physical device, owing to the inherent physical uniqueness of its hardware. This uniqueness arises from nanoscale variations in the integrated circuit chips during the manufacturing process. Typically, this physical distinctiveness is utilized to transmit a challenge string into the integrated circuit and generate a unique response string. The challenge-response behavior of a PUF resembles a mathematical function, where both the input (challenge) and output (response) are represented as bit strings. Mathematically, the PUF operation can be expressed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R = PUF\left( C \right)$$\end{document}, where C denotes the input challenge and R represents the corresponding output response. In summary, an ideal PUF should possess the following distinctive characteristics32:

The PUF response is contingent upon the individual physical disorder inherent in the design.It is straightforward to evaluate and implement.Different inputs yield outputs that resemble those of a random function, while identical inputs consistently produce the same outputs.Despite similar configurations, such as processors and architectures, PUFs can generate unique results.It is crucial to note that native PUFs lack inherent noise resistance. Current research focuses on developing robust and reliable PUF models that can function effectively under varying conditions, including voltage fluctuations and wide temperature ranges. In our study, we integrate a fuzzy extractor with PUFs to mitigate potential error impacts and enhance overall system reliability.

System model and adversary model

System model

Gateway (GW): GW is an intermediate data transmission device and serves as the sole entity facilitating data communications and verifying the identities of entities engaged in transactions.

User (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${U_i}$$\end{document}): users log in to their mobile devices using their identities, biometric information, and passwords, and obtain data transmitted from sensor nodes to GW with IoT. During the execution of the scheme, Users strictly adhere to the scheme process but may intentionally or unintentionally disclose intermediate results unrelated to their operations.

Sensor (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${S_j}$$\end{document}): given its role in assisting with obtaining data, the sensor is considered a critical terminal equipment to transmit data to GW. Each sensor is equipped with an embedded PUF device, which can be damaged but not stolen, enabling secure information transmission. Furthermore, the sensor needs to be registered on the GW before being installed on the terminal and requires authentication and key agreement operations with the communicating user.

Adversary model

In the proposed scheme, any entities (including those participating in the scheme) that deviate from their designated functions or exhibit improper behavior are classified as adversaries. Given that most schemes operate within an open wireless communication environment, the attack model encompasses both internal and external threats. External threats External threats arise from entities outside the communication framework who possess the capability to intercept and analyze communications between system entities over public networks. Such adversaries may deduce identities, locations, and potentially the content of transmitted information if they acquire the necessary decryption keys. Consequently, external threats involve attackers’ abilities to intercept, resend, read, modify, delete, delay, eavesdrop, impersonate legitimate entities, and disrupt session states with temporary secret values in public communication channels.Internal threats Internal threats stem from network components or entities within the system, particularly users engaging in illicit activities and sensors adhering to the “fair but curious” paradigm. For instance, while sensors comply with pre-established agreements, they may still collect sensitive information such as the identity of users. These internal adversaries pose significant risks to the reliability, privacy, and integrity of the system, necessitating robust security measures to counter potential attacks.

External threats External threats arise from entities outside the communication framework who possess the capability to intercept and analyze communications between system entities over public networks. Such adversaries may deduce identities, locations, and potentially the content of transmitted information if they acquire the necessary decryption keys. Consequently, external threats involve attackers’ abilities to intercept, resend, read, modify, delete, delay, eavesdrop, impersonate legitimate entities, and disrupt session states with temporary secret values in public communication channels.

External threats External threats arise from entities outside the communication framework who possess the capability to intercept and analyze communications between system entities over public networks. Such adversaries may deduce identities, locations, and potentially the content of transmitted information if they acquire the necessary decryption keys. Consequently, external threats involve attackers’ abilities to intercept, resend, read, modify, delete, delay, eavesdrop, impersonate legitimate entities, and disrupt session states with temporary secret values in public communication channels.

Internal threats Internal threats stem from network components or entities within the system, particularly users engaging in illicit activities and sensors adhering to the “fair but curious” paradigm. For instance, while sensors comply with pre-established agreements, they may still collect sensitive information such as the identity of users. These internal adversaries pose significant risks to the reliability, privacy, and integrity of the system, necessitating robust security measures to counter potential attacks.

For instance, while sensors comply with pre-established agreements, they may still collect sensitive information such as the identity of users. These internal adversaries pose significant risks to the reliability, privacy, and integrity of the system, necessitating robust security measures to counter potential attacks.

Proposed scheme

This section proposes a mutual authentication and key agreement scheme for a wireless sensor network. In this scheme, both users and sensor nodes need to register with the gateway node, and the detailed authentication procedure is as follows:

System initialization phase

In this phase, the gateway node GW is in charge of initializing some relevant parameters for the system and performs the following steps: GW first generates a large prime p and a random value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \in \left( { - \infty , + \infty } \right)$$\end{document} as the seed of the Chebyshew polynomial. Second, it randomly chooses two one-way hash functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${H_1},{H_2}$$\end{document}, a symmetric encryption/ decryption function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$En{c_k}\left( \cdot \right) /De{c_k}\left( \cdot \right)$$\end{document}. Third, it generates its identity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I{D_{GW}}$$\end{document}, its master private key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s{k_{GW}}$$\end{document}, and the corresponding public key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p{k_{GW}} = {\Gamma _{s{k_{GW}}}}\left( \omega \right) \bmod p$$\end{document}. Subsequently, GW broadcasts the global parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ {p,\omega ,p{k_{GW}},{H_1},{H_2},En{c_k}\left( \cdot \right) /De{c_k}\left( \cdot \right) } \right\}$$\end{document}.

Registration phase

To securely transmit data in the IoT network, the user needs to register in a designated system, which may be assumed to be an APP developed by the gateway. The registration phase revolves around two entities: 1) User and 2) Sensor node registration phases, and the gateway node plays a vital role as a trusted intermediary between the user node and sensor node. This phase is performed via a secure channel.

{I{D_{GW}}\left\| {I{D_i}} \right. } \right) \oplus s{k_{GW}}$$\end{document}. Then, it responds with credentials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ {PI{D_i},{M_i},{N_i}} \right\}$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${U_i}$$\end{document} via a secure channel. Step 2: Upon receiving the registration request, GW evaluates the short-term pseudo identity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {s{k_{GW}}\left\| {{\alpha _i}} \right. } \right)$$\end{document}, the auxiliary parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\| {s{k_{GW}}\| {I{D_{GW}}} })$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${M_i} = {X_i} \oplus$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${H_2}( {I{D_i}\| {{R_{i0}}} } ) \oplus {\alpha _i}$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${N_i} = {H_2}( PI{D_i}\| {{X_i}}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${E_i} = {X_i} \oplus {H_2}\left( Sensor node registration phase

Assume that a sensor node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{N_j}$$\end{document} transmits its registration request information to the GW. After the registration phase, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{N_j}$$\end{document} will be deployed to conveniently provide data acquisition services for the IoT. The following detailed steps are given for the registration.Step 1: Each sensor node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{N_j}$$\end{document} selects a unique identity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I{D_j}$$\end{document}, random nonce \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C_{j0}} \in {\left\{ {0,1} \right\} ^k}$$\end{document}, and CRP \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {{R_{j0}},{C_{j0}}} \right)$$\end{document} with its PUF device, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${R_{j0}} = PUF\left( {{C_{j0}}} \right)$$\end{document}. After that, it generates a temporary private key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s{k_j} = {H_1}\left( {I{D_j}\left\| {{C_{j0}}} \right. } \right)$$\end{document} and corresponding public key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p{k_j} = {\Gamma _{s{k_j}}}\left( \omega \right) \bmod p$$\end{document}. At last, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{N_j}$$\end{document} transmits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ {I{D_j},{R_{j0}}} \right\}$$\end{document} to GW securely.Step 2: Upon receiving a request message, GW checks whether \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I{D_j}$$\end{document} is a new identity by retrieving its database. If it is a new identity, GW generates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Q_j} = {H_2}\left( {I{D_j}\left\| {I{D_{GW}}} \right. \left\| {s{k_{GW}}} \right. } \right)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$HI{D_j} = I{D_j} \oplus {H_2}\left( {s{k_{GW}}\left\| {{Q_i}} \right. } \right)$$\end{document}, and sends \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ {HI{D_j},{Q_j}} \right\}$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{N_j}$$\end{document} securely. Finally, it deletes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Q_j}$$\end{document} and stores credentials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ {HI{D_j},I{D_j},{R_{j0}}} \right\}$$\end{document} in its database.Step 3: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{N_j}$$\end{document} receives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Q_j}$$\end{document} and stores \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ {HI{D_j},{Q_j},{C_{j0}}} \right\}$$\end{document} locally to finish the registration phase.

Step 1: Each sensor node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{N_j}$$\end{document} selects a unique identity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I{D_j}$$\end{document}, random nonce \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C_{j0}} \in {\left\{ {0,1} \right\} ^k}$$\end{document}, and CRP \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {{R_{j0}},{C_{j0}}} \right)$$\end{document} with its PUF device, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${R_{j0}} = PUF\left( {{C_{j0}}} \right)$$\end{document}. After that, it generates a temporary private key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s{k_j} = {H_1}\left( {I{D_j}\left\| {{C_{j0}}} \right. } \right)$$\end{document} and corresponding public key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p{k_j} = {\Gamma _{s{k_j}}}\left( \omega \right) \bmod p$$\end{document}. At last, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{N_j}$$\end{document} transmits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ {I{D_j},{R_{j0}}} \right\}$$\end{document} to GW securely.

Step 1: Each sensor node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{N_j}$$\end{document} selects a unique identity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I{D_j}$$\end{document}, random nonce \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${C_{j0}} \in {\left\{ {0,1} \right\} ^k}$$\end{document}, and CRP \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {{R_{j0}},{C_{j0}}} \right)$$\end{document} with its PUF device, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${R_{j0}} = PUF\left( {{C_{j0}}} \right)$$\end{document}. After that, it generates a temporary private key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s{k_j} = {H_1}\left( {I{D_j}\left\| {{C_{j0}}} \right. } \right)$$\end{document} and corresponding public key \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p{k_j} = {\Gamma _{s{k_j}}}\left( \omega \right) \bmod p$$\end{document}. At last, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{N_j}$$\end{document} transmits \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ {I{D_j},{R_{j0}}} \right\}$$\end{document} to GW securely.

Step 2: Upon receiving a request message, GW checks whether \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I{D_j}$$\end{document} is a new identity by retrieving its database. Finally, it deletes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Q_j}$$\end{document} and stores credentials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ {HI{D_j},I{D_j},{R_{j0}}} \right\}$$\end{document} in its database.

Step 2: Upon receiving a request message, GW checks whether \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I{D_j}$$\end{document} is a new identity by retrieving its database. If it is a new identity, GW generates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Q_j} = {H_2}\left( {I{D_j}\left\| {I{D_{GW}}} \right. \left\| {s{k_{GW}}} \right. } \right)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$HI{D_j} = I{D_j} \oplus {H_2}\left( {s{k_{GW}}\left\| {{Q_i}} \right. } \right)$$\end{document}, and sends \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ {HI{D_j},{Q_j}} \right\}$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{N_j}$$\end{document} securely. Step 3: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S{N_j}$$\end{document} receives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${Q_j}$$\end{document} and stores \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ {HI{D_j},{Q_j},{C_{j0}}} \right\}$$\end{document} locally to finish the registration phase.

Authentication and key agreement phase

This phase involves identity authentication and session key agreement among the User (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${U_i}$$\end{document}), Sensor node (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${S{N_j}}$$\end{document}), and Gateway node (GW). At the same time, it is assumed that the clocks of users, sensors and gateways are precisely synchronized during the “Authentication and Key Agreement Phase”. For simplicity, we assume that the identity of the user who needs to connect the sensor node is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I{D_i}$$\end{document} and the sensor node’s identity is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I{D_j}$$\end{document}. The detailed key agreement is implemented as follows:Step 1: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${U_i} \rightarrow GW:Ms{g_1} = \left\{ {PI{D_i},ms{g_1},{T_1}} \right\}$$\end{document} User \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${U_i}$$\end{document} runs the APP installed in its smart device along with its login credentials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ID_i^*$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$BIO_i^*$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PW_i^*$$\end{document}.
